# ROUND 2/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: score += 1 for every stationary robot (penalizes stopping)
- After switch time: score += 1 for every moving robot (penalizes moving)
- Switch happens between steps 400-600; a green wall lights up at switch time
- Optimal behavior: move before switch, stop after switch
- The green wall (color 2 = green? Let me check: clr colors are {1,2,3,4,5,6}) is the signal

The key insight: robots should MOVE before switch time, then STOP when they see green. Green wall lights up at switch → robots should detect green (C=7, color 2 for green) and transition to Stop.

**Best performer analysis:**
- Score 12211.88: Complex 4-state machine
- Score 12285.07: Simply stop always (1-state Stop) - surprisingly good! This suggests stopping is often better (run is 1200 steps, switch at 400-600, so ~600-800 steps of stopping phase vs 400-600 steps of moving phase)
- Wait: if switch is at ~500 steps average, then 500 steps of moving phase (score += robots moving) and 700 steps of stopping phase (score += robots stationary). So stopping is penalized for 700 steps and moving penalized for 700 steps... no wait.

Let me re-read: before switch, score += stationary robots; after switch, score += moving robots.
- All-stop: penalized 500 steps × 24 = 12000 for first phase, 0 for second = ~12000
- All-move: 0 for first phase, 700 × 24 = 16800 for second = ~16800
- Optimal: move first phase (0), stop second phase (0) = 0

So the champion (12211) is close to all-stop (12285). The robots need to detect green and keep moving until then.

**Strategy:** State 0 = Exploration (move), transition to Stop when green detected. The green wall should be color 2 (green = 2? Colors: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan based on cle colors - but clr for GoToColor/GoAwayColor might use same mapping).

**My machines:**

1. **Champion (survival)** - exact copy
2. **Exploitation of champion** - small tweaks
3. **Simple 2-state**: Explore → Stop on seeing green (color 2)
4. **Simple 2-state**: Explore → Stop on seeing green with different params
5. **3-state**: Explore, GoToColor(green), Stop
6. Various explorations

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 2:**
- The champion (10771.15) is a 2-state machine: Attraction → Stop, triggered by seeing green (color 2) with p=0.9, with a tiny random-stop escape; Stop state has tiny random-transition back to Attraction and a small green-detection back to Stop.
- 2nd and 3rd best are simple 2-state: Explore → Stop on green, with p=0.9 or 0.95
- The pattern is clear: **Move in state 0, detect green (color 2) with high probability → go to Stop**
- 3-state machines with extra complexity performed WORSE

**What to exploit:**
1. The detection probability for green (p=0.9, 0.95) - try values from 0.8 to 0.99
2. The tiny back-transition probability from Stop - try near 0 (0.0005, 0.001, 0.002)
3. The small green-detection in Stop state (p=0.05) - try removing or varying
4. Attraction gain in champion - try 1.5, 2.0, 3.0, 4.0
5. Exploration rwm in 2nd/3rd best - try 20, 40, 60, 80
6. Try color 4 (yellow) for robot LEDs to help robots find each other

**Key insight:** The stop state should almost never go back to moving. The move state should aggressively transition to stop when green is seen.

**Strategy:**
- 1 survival (champion exact copy)
- ~6 exploitation of top 3
- ~7 exploration with different topologies

For color 2 = green (the wall that lights up). The robots need to see the green wall. GoToColor(green) might help robots see green faster.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 3:**
- Champion (7370.68): GoToColor(green=2) → Stop when seeing green (p=0.9), with tiny random escape (p=0.001). Stop state has tiny random back-transition (p=0.0005).
- The GoToColor behavior is dramatically better than Attraction or Exploration for the moving phase! It actively moves toward the green wall, so when the green light appears, the robot is already oriented toward it AND can detect it faster.
- 2nd best uses Exploration(rwm=20) - slow turning is good for detecting green
- 3rd-5th use Attraction with various gains

**What to exploit:**
1. GoToColor(green) is the clear winner for state 0 - vary detection probability (0.85, 0.9, 0.95)
2. The random escape from state 0 (p=0.001) - try 0.0005, 0.002
3. The Stop state back-transition (p=0.0005) - keep very low
4. Try adding a second condition to Stop state (e.g., green detection to go back to GoToColor - but this might be bad)
5. Try GoToColor with different colors to spread robots before green appears
6. Try 3-state: GoToColor(green) → Stop, but also explore other states

**Key insight on p=0.99 being worse:** Too high green detection probability is bad - it might cause false positives or the robot can't move effectively. p=0.9 seems optimal.

**Why GoToColor(green) works:** Before the switch, the robot moves toward green (which doesn't exist yet, so it just moves around). When green appears, it moves toward the green wall AND detects it with p=0.9, transitioning to Stop.

Let me also try GoToColor with other initial colors that might spread robots better before green appears, then switch to GoToColor(green) when seen.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 4:**
- Champion (6675.39): GoToColor(green) → Stop, with p=0.9 green detection, p=0.001 random escape from move state, p=0.0002 random escape from stop state
- The key difference from previous champion: p=0.0002 (2e-04) in stop state vs 5e-04. Lower back-transition probability from Stop is better!
- 3-state machines performed much worse - keep it simple 2-state
- Adding extra conditions to state 0 (yellow detection) was harmful
- The core structure is locked in: GoToColor(2) → Stop on green, very low escape from stop

**Parameter analysis:**
- p0x0 (green detection in move state): 0.9 seems optimal (0.95 was worse in round 3)
- p0x1 (random escape from move): 0.001 seems good
- p1x0 (random escape from stop): 0.0002 is best so far, 0.0005 is slightly worse
- The stop LED color doesn't matter much (cle1=4 vs 5 gave same score)

**What to try:**
1. Vary p1x0 around 0.0002: try 0.0001, 0.00015, 0.0003, 0.0
2. Vary p0x1 around 0.001: try 0.0005, 0.002, 0.0008
3. Vary p0x0 around 0.9: try 0.88, 0.92, 0.87
4. Try p1x0 = 0 effectively (but we can't set 0, minimum is 0.0001 or similar)
5. Try GoAwayColor(green=2) in stop state to reinforce staying (no - stop is already stop)
6. Add green detection condition to stop state - if robot sees green again, stay stopped (but stop already stays)

**Key question:** Can we push p1x0 even lower? The champion has 2e-04. Try 1e-04, 5e-05.
Also try p0x0 = 0.9 but add a second green detection condition with slightly different probability as a compound trigger.

**Insight:** The stop state escape at p=0.0002 means in expectation, a robot escapes every 5000 steps. Run is 1200 steps. So effectively robots almost never escape stop. Going even lower (1e-04) might help marginally.

## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 6:**
- Champion unchanged at 6279.66 - we're in a narrow optimization zone
- p0x1 (random escape from GoToColor state): 0.002 is champion, but 0.0015 and 0.001 also competitive. Values >0.002 (0.0025, 0.003, 0.004) are progressively worse. The sweet spot seems between 0.001-0.002.
- p1x0 (random escape from stop): 1e-04 gave 6607 (2nd best in this round), 2e-04 is champion, 3e-04 gave 6708. Lower is better but with noise.
- p0x0 (green detection): 0.85 (4th best) and 0.9 (champion) both work. 0.88 was 4th in round 5.

**What to try:**
1. Combine p0x1=0.0015 with p1x0=1e-04 (two independently good findings)
2. Combine p0x1=0.002 with p1x0=1e-04
3. Try p0x1=0.0017, 0.0018 (between 0.0015 and 0.002)
4. Try p1x0=5e-05 (even lower than 1e-04)
5. Try p0x0=0.87, 0.86 (between 0.85 and 0.88)
6. Try p0x1=0.001 with p1x0=1e-04
7. Add a small gray-floor condition to stop state (if robot is on gray, it's not near the wall - maybe helps? But floor is uniformly gray so this doesn't help)
8. The score is noisy - re-test champion-neighborhood combinations

**Key observation:** The worst performers have p0x1 ≥ 0.0025 - too much random exploration hurts. The best range for p0x1 is 0.001-0.002. For p1x0, lower is better (1e-04 < 2e-04).

## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 7:**
- Champion (6224.60): p0x1=0.002, p1x0=1e-04. Confirmed p1x0=1e-04 is better than 2e-04.
- 2nd best (6239.63): p0x1=0.0012, p1x0=1e-04 - very close! Sweet spot for p0x1 is between 0.0012-0.002
- 3rd (6273.52): Same as champion but with extra green detection in stop state (p=0.005) - negligibly worse
- 4th (6323.67): p0x1=0.0015, p1x0=1e-04
- 5th (6336.68): p0x0=0.87, p0x1=0.0015, p1x0=1e-04
- p0x1=0.001 was worst among the good machines (6583) - too low!

**Parameter optimization:**
- p0x1 sweet spot: 0.0012-0.002 (0.001 too low, 0.0025+ too high)
- p1x0 sweet spot: 1e-04 (confirmed better than 2e-04, need to try 5e-05)
- p0x0: 0.87-0.9 range competitive

**Plan:**
1. Survival: champion exact copy
2. Exploit: try p0x1 in {0.0013, 0.0014, 0.0016, 0.0018, 0.0020} with p1x0=1e-04
3. Exploit: try p1x0 in {5e-05, 7e-05} with champion p0x1=0.002
4. Exploit: combine p0x0=0.87-0.88 with p0x1=0.0012-0.0015
5. Try p0x1=0.002 with p1x0=1e-04 but varying p0x0 (0.86, 0.88, 0.92)

## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 8:**
- Champion unchanged at 6224.60 - the scores this round were higher (noisy evaluation)
- Best this round: p0x1=0.0014, p1x0=1e-04 (6408) - slightly lower than champion's 0.002
- p0x0=0.88 and 0.87 both gave ~6454 with p0x1=0.002, p1x0=1e-04
- p0x0=0.92 gave 6475 - slightly worse than 0.9
- p0x1=0.0013 was the worst among good machines (6626) - confirming 0.0014 is near the lower boundary

**Parameter landscape summary:**
- p0x1: sweet spot appears to be 0.0014-0.002. Try 0.0015, 0.0016, 0.0018, 0.002
- p1x0: 1e-04 confirmed best. Try 5e-05, 7e-05 again
- p0x0: 0.87-0.92 all competitive, 0.9 slightly best historically

**Key insight:** The scores are quite noisy between runs (same machine gives 6224 in round 7 but 6467 in round 8). We should focus on the best parameter combinations and repeat them with slight variations to find robust improvements.

**Strategy:**
- Keep champion exact
- Try combinations of p0x1 ∈ {0.0014, 0.0015, 0.0016, 0.002} × p1x0 ∈ {5e-05, 7e-05, 1e-04}
- Try p0x0 ∈ {0.87, 0.88, 0.9} combinations
- One exploration: try adding neighbor condition to help robots cluster for better green detection

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 24 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.38 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
